XV.3
Magic factory auxiliary operations

The magic state factories require a number of auxiliary non-Clifford operations performed at the logical level. In this section we show how to implement these operations using the logical instruction set supplemented by physical non-Clifford operations.
Here the only non-Clifford ingredient is the preparation of a physical
|H\rangle
state.
We refer to such constructions as
IS+H-based
.

XV.3.1
Logical
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
gates

This subsection describes IS+H-based implementations of the logical gates

R_{y}(\pi/4)
and
R_{y}(-\pi/4)
used below in the magic-state factory
constructions.

Figure

43
shows the IS+H-based
implementations of logical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
and
R_{y}(-\pi/4)
used below:

Fig.

43(a)
implements
R_{y}(\pi/4)
and

Fig.

43(b)
implements
R_{y}(-\pi/4)
.
These are obtained from the
IS+H-based
T
-gate circuit of

Fig.

8
(b) by conjugation with
SH
,
using the identity
R_{y}(\pi/4)=SHTH^{\dagger}S^{\dagger}
.

(a)

(b)

Figure 43
:
IS+H-based implementations of logical
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
. (a) A physical ancilla prepared in the
|H\rangle
state, a joint
Y\bar{Y}
measurement, and an
X
-basis ancilla readout realize logical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
up to a Pauli frame update. (b) The corresponding IS+H-based circuit for logical
R_{y}(-\pi/4)
derived from the
R_{y}(\pi/4)
implementation.

XV.3.2
Physical H-State injection

Both of our magic state factories begin by preparing two
|\bar{H}\rangle
states in a code block.
Preparing a single logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
state can be done by implementing a logical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
gate using a
physical ancilla prepared in the
|H\rangle
state to provide the non-Clifford resource.
The circuit
Fig.

45
demonstrates the implementation
of the IS+H-based circuit for this in a code block.
Since
|H\rangle=R_{y}(\pi/4)|0\rangle
, we first prepare the physical
ancilla by applying a physical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
rotation. We then measure the
joint
Y\bar{Y}
operator between this physical ancilla and the target logical
qubit. Because this hybrid measurement couples an unencoded qubit to a
code block, it is implemented as an
\mathrm{EDM}
(see
Section

XIII.1
); although
the code block can undergo SEC between repeated rounds, the unencoded ancilla
cannot, so a single ancilla fault can correlate the repeated
Y\bar{Y}
outcomes
and invalidate an
\mathrm{ECM}
majority vote or Viterbi-measurement protocol run. Finally, the ancilla is measured
in the
X

basis, and the resulting Pauli and Clifford byproduct corrections are absorbed
into the software frame via Clifford frame tracking rather than applied
physically; see
Section

VI.3
.

After preparing the logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
state via this injection circuit, we

\bar{H}
-twirl it by implementing the logical Hadamard with probability
1/2
via frame tracking.
The
\bar{H}
-twirl leaves the injected state in a stochastic mixture of
\bar{H}
and
\bar{Y}\bar{H}
(Ref.
131
for proof), so that the relevant first-order logical fault afterwards is an undetected logical
\bar{Y}
.

Even without
\bar{H}
-twirling, errors that do not correspond to an undetected logical
\bar{Y}
fault are disregarded under the ideal-logical-operation approximation of
Section

XV.1
, or contribute only through the hybrid-
\mathrm{EDM}
logical-outcome error
p_{\mathrm{EDM}r}
, which is not first order in
p
. This is relevant later when studying faults in EDM-based
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
gates inside controlled-
\bar{H}
constructions, as these gates cannot be twirled easily.

Definition 9

(Injected
\bar{Y}
fault)
.

Consider the
\mathrm{EDM}
-
r

H
-state injection gadget of
Fig.

45
. An injected
\bar{Y}
fault is an undetected logical
\bar{Y}
fault on the prepared logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
state induced by a fault in the
H
-state injection circuit. We denote its probability by
q_{Y}
.

Heuristic 3

(H-state injection
\bar{Y}
fault rate)
.

For the
\mathrm{EDM}
-
r

H
-state injection gadget, we estimate the
injected-
\bar{Y}
-fault probability by

\displaystyle q_{Y}\approx{}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{8}{15}+\frac{7r}{15}\right)p

(20)

\displaystyle+(r+1)\,\mathrm{Depth}(\mathrm{SEC})\frac{p}{150}.

Under the magic-factory error model of

Section

XV.1
, we disregard memory errors on the
target code block when applying this heuristic.
We refer to
Eq.

20
as the
H
-state injection

\bar{Y}
fault rate heuristic
.

Figure 44
:
Fault locations contributing to an injected
\bar{Y}
fault in the
H
-state injection gadget. Here, the joint
Y\bar{Y}
measurement is implemented as
\mathrm{EDM}
-
2
(that is,
r=2
). The figure represents
H
-state injection from the perspective of the single cat-state qubit
C0
aligned with the physical ancilla
P0
.
C1..Cw
represents the rest of the cat state carrying out the logical measurement of
\bar{Y}
on the logical qubit target (
L0
). The bright red Pauli operators mark the location of all effective Pauli errors that lead to an injected
\bar{Y}
fault for each source of root Pauli errors in items 1) through 6) of the justification of
3
.

Justification.

The heuristic is obtained by accounting for first-order faults that can leave an
undetected
\bar{Y}
on the injected logical state. Under the ideal-logical-operation approximation
of
Section

XV.1
, we disregard memory faults on
the target code block and also neglect the separate logical-outcome error of the hybrid
Y\bar{Y}

\mathrm{EDM}
itself.
For the
r=3
injection
\mathrm{EDM}
used below
(
5
and
12
), the heuristic

Eq.

16
gives a residual accepted-outcome error of order

10^{-8}
for both factories, far below the first-order ancilla-fault
terms retained below, so to leading order it suffices to track only the
effective Pauli left on the physical ancilla.
In the injection gadget of
Fig.

45
, the final ancilla

X
measurement determines whether a logical
\bar{Y}
frame update is applied to
the target logical qubit. First-order faults that anti-commute with the repeated
Y\bar{Y}
check are
rejected by the
\mathrm{EDM}
, and faults on the cat qubit or on the ancilla-cat
entangling gate matter only through the ancilla Pauli they induce after
propagation. Therefore, the undetected first-order faults contributing to an undetected
logical
\bar{Y}
fault are exactly those whose net effect is an accepted
Y
or
Z
fault on
the physical ancilla immediately before the final ancilla
X
readout,
because either fault flips that readout and hence triggers the wrong
logical-
\bar{Y}
frame update.

The fault locations and coefficients below were obtained by an exhaustive
computational search over the moving-qubit noise model of

Sections

IV.3
and
3
. For each
elementary noise source, we inserted each Pauli fault, propagated it
through the remainder of the gadget to the final measurements, and counted
it as an undetected injected-
\bar{Y}
fault exactly when it left all
repeated
Y\bar{Y}
outcomes unchanged but flipped the final
X
measurement of
the physical ancilla.
In the moving-qubit model (
Table

3
),
single-qubit preparation, one-qubit gates, and measurement readout faults
occur with rate
p/10
, idle Pauli faults occur with rate
p/100
, and the
cat-state entangling gate in one cat-based
Y\bar{Y}
measurement is a
two-qubit gate with depolarizing rate
p
. We also use
the cat-state noise model of
Section

XIII.2
, which treats the
relevant cat qubit as depolarized with rate
p
. Under the loss/leakage
convention of
Section

XV.1
, loss or leakage on
the physical ancilla during the idle interval before the final
X
readout
causes a detected restart rather than an injected
\bar{Y}
fault. Hence these
events do not contribute to
q_{Y}
; instead, they are included in the retry
rate of the injection gadget, as stated in
4
.

We now list all fault locations that can lead to an injected
\bar{Y}
fault.

Figure

44
summarizes the effective fault locations
for the six Pauli error sources counted below.

1.

an effective
Y
fault on the physical ancilla before the first
cat-based
Y\bar{Y}
measurement, arising either from the noisy
|0\rangle

preparation or from the subsequent physical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
gate, with
total first-order probability
p/15+p/30=p/10
. For the
preparation term, the moving-qubit noise model gives a single-qubit
depolarizing fault of rate
p/10
, so each Pauli branch occurs with
probability
p/30
. Of these three branches,
Z
acts trivially on

|0\rangle
, while
X|0\rangle
and
Y|0\rangle
both equal
|1\rangle
up to
phase, so after the subsequent physical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
they yield the
same effective ancilla-
Y
fault. Thus preparation
contributes
2(p/30)=p/15
. By contrast, for the post-gate
depolarizing fault after the physical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
, only the literal

Y
branch contributes to this item, giving
p/30
. This ancilla
Y

fault commutes with every repeated
Y\bar{Y}
check, so the
\mathrm{EDM}
accepts, but
its
Z
component anticommutes with the final ancilla
X
readout and
flips that bit;

2.

an
X
fault on the cat-state qubit that couples to the physical

|H\rangle
ancilla in any one of the
r
cat-based
Y\bar{Y}
measurements,
contributing at most
rp/3
. Here the cat qubit is modeled as a
single-qubit depolarizing channel of rate
p
, and only the
X

component can produce an undetected injected-
\bar{Y}
fault: a

Y=iXZ
fault or
Z
fault has a
Z
component that flips the extracted bit and
causes the
\mathrm{EDM}
to be rejected;

3.

a
Y
fault on the physical
|H\rangle
state induced by the
cat-based entangling gate in any one of the
r

Y\bar{Y}
measurements,
contributing at most
2rp/15
. The entangling-gate fault is uniformly
distributed over the
15
non-identity two-qubit Pauli errors. For
this count, we place the fault immediately after the ancilla-cat
entangling gate of a given
Y\bar{Y}
round and propagate it through the
remainder of that round, including the cat-qubit
X
readout and the
conditioned Clifford updates in
Fig.

45
. Writing
the post-gate Pauli fault in ancilla-cat order, the four faults
YI
,

YX
,
YY
, and
YZ
yield an effective
Y
on the physical

|H\rangle
ancilla after that propagation. Of these, only
YI
and

YX
are accepted in that round:
YY
and
YZ
flip the extracted

Y\bar{Y}
bit and are therefore rejected by the repeated measurement. The
accepted branches are still harmful because, once the fault has
propagated to an ancilla
Y
, it commutes with every subsequent
Y\bar{Y}

check and so is not caught by the
\mathrm{EDM}
, while its
Z
component
anticommutes with the final ancilla
X
readout and flips that
outcome, producing the injected-
\bar{Y}
fault;

4.

a
Z
fault on the physical
|H\rangle
state after the last
cat-based
Y\bar{Y}
measurement, with probability
5p/15=p/3
. Here we
focus on propagated ancilla-
Z
faults because the corresponding
propagated ancilla-
Y
faults were already counted in item 3. An effective
Z
on the physical ancilla flips the final
ancilla
X
readout, but if it occurs in the last
Y\bar{Y}
round there is
no later repeated measurement through which the
\mathrm{EDM}
can catch it on
the physical ancilla; at that stage, the
\mathrm{EDM}
can still reject only
residual faults on the cat state before that cat qubit is measured.
After placing the two-qubit Pauli fault immediately after the ancilla-cat entangling gate
and propagating it through the rest of that round,
11
non-identity
Pauli terms are equivalent to an effective
Z
on the physical ancilla
before the final ancilla
X
readout. Of these, only the five branches
with cat Pauli
I
or
X
are accepted in the final
\mathrm{EDM}
round; the other
six have cat Pauli
Y
or
Z
, flip the final cat-qubit
X
readout,
and are therefore rejected by the
\mathrm{EDM}
. The surviving effective
Z

flips the final ancilla
X
readout. If the same accepted
Z
fault
arises in any non-final
Y\bar{Y}
round, it is caught by a subsequent
repeated
Y\bar{Y}
measurement; therefore only a fault in the last
Y\bar{Y}

round contributes here, giving
5p/15
;

5.

a flip of the final
X
-basis readout, with probability
p/10
;

6.

a
Y
or
Z
fault on the idle physical
|H\rangle
state before the final

X
readout. Since the moving-qubit idle noise is a single-qubit
depolarizing channel of rate
p/100
, only the
Y
and
Z
components
flip the final ancilla
X
readout, so the relevant first-order rate is

(2/3)(p/100)=p/150
. This contributes at most

(r+1)\,\mathrm{Depth}(\mathrm{SEC})\,p/150
;

Summing the non-idle contributions in items 1–5 gives

\frac{p}{10}+r\frac{p}{3}+r\frac{2p}{15}+\frac{p}{3}+\frac{p}{10}=\left(\frac{8}{15}+\frac{7r}{15}\right)p.

Adding the idle-noise contribution from item 6,

(r+1)\,\mathrm{Depth}(\mathrm{SEC})\frac{p}{150},

to this non-idle sum yields

Eq.

20
as the resulting first-order estimate.
∎

Heuristic 4

(Retry rate of
H
-state injection)
.

Let
p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}
denote the probability that the
\mathrm{EDM}
-
r

H
-state injection gadget aborts and must be restarted. Under the magic-factory error model of
Section

XV.1
, we estimate the retry rate by

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle\approx 1-\bigl((1-p_{\text{flip}})^{r}+p_{\text{flip}}^{r}\bigr)(1-p_{\mathrm{anc,det}}),

(21)

\displaystyle p_{\text{flip}}

\displaystyle\approx C_{1}wp,

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{anc,det}}

\displaystyle\approx(r+1)\,\mathrm{Depth}(\mathrm{SEC})\,(p_{\text{loss}}+p_{\text{leak}}).

Here,
p_{\text{flip}}
is the single-round cat-based outcome-flip probability from

Eq.

15
, applied to the hybrid

Y\bar{Y}
measurement with weight
w
.

Justification.

By
Definition

3
, an
\mathrm{EDM}
-
r
accepts only if all
r
extracted bits agree.
Therefore the
\mathrm{EDM}
outcomes are accepted in exactly two disjoint cases:
either all
r
outcomes are correct, with probability
(1-p_{\text{flip}})^{r}
, or
all
r
outcomes are flipped, with probability
p_{\text{flip}}^{r}
.

By the ancilla loss/leakage convention of

Section

XV.1
, any loss or leakage on the
physical ancilla during the idle interval before the final
X
readout
causes a detected restart rather than an injected
\bar{Y}
fault. Using the
idle loss and leakage rates from
Table

3
,
the probability of such a detected loss/leakage event is

p_{\mathrm{anc,det}}\approx(r+1)\,\mathrm{Depth}(\mathrm{SEC})\,(p_{\text{loss}}+p_{\text{leak}}).

Approximating this ancilla-detection event as independent of the cat-based
outcome flips, the gadget is accepted if and only if the
\mathrm{EDM}
outcomes
agree and no detected loss/leakage event occurs. Hence the total acceptance
probability is estimated by

\bigl((1-p_{\text{flip}})^{r}+p_{\text{flip}}^{r}\bigr)(1-p_{\mathrm{anc,det}}),

and taking the complement gives the heuristic estimate

Eq.

21
.
∎

Figure 45
:
H
-state injection from a physical ancilla
P0
onto a logical qubit
L0
, implemented in a code block with SEC-level scheduling. This realizes the IS+H-based circuit for logical
R_{y}(\pi/4)
, with the hybrid joint
Y\bar{Y}
measurement implemented as an
\mathrm{EDM}
; the
r
marker in the diagram denotes that the
\mathrm{EDM}
uses a variable number
r
of rounds.

XV.3.3
Logical Controlled-
\bar{H}
gate

In this subsection, we discuss the IS+H-based implementation of a logical controlled-
\bar{H}
gate, which is used in the verification stage of the
\mathrm{CH2}
protocol.
The construction is based on the standard decomposition of a controlled-Hadamard gate into a controlled-
\bar{Z}
gate and the
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
gates discussed above.

Figure

46
shows an IS+H-based implementation of a logical controlled-
\bar{H}
gate.
The construction uses the standard identity

\mathrm{C}H=(I\otimes R_{y}(\pi/4))\,\mathrm{CZ}\,(I\otimes R_{y}(-\pi/4)),

as in Fig. 3 of Ref.
52
. In code blocks where all logical
Pauli operators are accessible, such as the
\mathrm{MEK}
factory in the
\mathrm{Q{70}}

code, the logical controlled-
\bar{Z}
is an accessible Clifford gate and can therefore be
implemented by frame tracking rather than expanded into instruction-set
operations. By contrast, the logical
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
gates are non-Clifford
and here require injected
|H\rangle
states, so they must be expanded into
their IS+H-based implementations
(
Fig.

43(a)
and

Fig.

43(b)
), yielding

Fig.

46
.

Figure 46
:
A logical controlled-
\bar{H}
gate constructed from the decomposition
\mathrm{C}\bar{H}=(I\otimes R_{y}(\pi/4))\,\mathrm{CZ}\,(I\otimes R_{y}(-\pi/4))
. The logical
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
gates are implemented using IS+H-based circuits. The intermediate logical
\mathrm{CZ}
is left unexpanded, since as a Clifford gate it can be implemented via the logical instruction set or by frame tracking.

Combining the logical decomposition of

Fig.

46
with the IS+H-based implementations of

R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
gives the corresponding code-block implementation of the
logical controlled-
\bar{H}
gate with SEC-level scheduling, shown in

Fig.

47
. Each logical
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
subcircuit uses a physical ancilla prepared in
|H\rangle

and an
\mathrm{EDM}
for the hybrid
Y\bar{Y}
measurement. The
R_{y}(\pi/4)
code-block implementation can be seen in
Fig.

45
.

Figure 47
:
Implementation of the logical controlled-
\bar{H}
gate in a code block with SEC-level scheduling. Each logical
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
subcircuit is realized by the
H
-state injection gadget of
Fig.

45
, which uses a physical ancilla prepared in
|H\rangle
and an
\mathrm{EDM}
for the hybrid
Y\bar{Y}
measurement.

XV.3.4
Transversal H measurement

For a code with strongly transversal Hadamard, the cat-based measurement shown in
Fig.

48
realizes the controlled-
M
measurement gadget of
77
for the Hermitian unitary
M=H^{\otimes n}
. Each physical controlled-
H
in this layer can be implemented by the physical gate sequence:
R_{y}(\pi/4,\text{target})\mathrm{CZ}(\text{control},\text{target})R_{y}(-\pi/4,\text{target})
. Starting from a cat ancilla and a data state
|\psi\rangle
, the transversal controlled-
H
layer prepares

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(|0\rangle^{\otimes n}|\psi\rangle+|1\rangle^{\otimes n}H^{\otimes n}|\psi\rangle\right).

Measuring the cat state in the
X
basis then projects the data onto the
\pm 1
eigenspaces of
M
, equivalently applying the projector
(I\pm M)/2
. Since
H^{\otimes n}=\bar{H}^{\otimes k}
on the code space, this gadget measures the joint logical observable
\bar{H}^{\otimes k}
. This is the verification primitive used by the
\mathrm{CH2}
protocol to verify the encoded
|\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{H}\rangle
state.

Figure 48
:
Cat-based measurement of the physical Hermitian unitary
H^{\otimes n}
in a code with strongly transversal Hadamard, yielding a measurement of the joint logical observable
\bar{H}^{\otimes k}
. Every data qubit in the code block is targeted by a physical controlled-H gate controlled by a separate cat state qubit.

XV.4
The CH2 factory

XV.4.1
The CH2 factory circuit

The
\mathrm{CH2}
factory realizes the
\mathrm{CH2}
protocol in the
\mathrm{Q{54}}
code. The protocol prepares a logical
|\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{H}\rangle
state and then verifies that state by measuring the joint logical observable
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
. Because the code admits transversal Hadamard, the factory can implement this verification fault-tolerantly with the cat-based measurement of
Fig.

48
. This is considerably cheaper than realizing the same check through a logical controlled-
\bar{H}
measurement, and does not require additional physical
|H\rangle
states.

The code-block schedule, shown in
Fig.

49
, first applies the
H
-state injection gadget of
Fig.

45
to logical qubit
0
, and then applies the same injection circuit to logical qubit
1
. These injections are implemented with
\mathrm{EDM}
-
3
measurements, as estimated in
5
. The factory then verifies the candidate
|\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{H}\rangle
state by measuring the joint logical observable
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
with the transversal gadget of
Fig.

48
, implemented as a weight-
54
Viterbi measurement with average runtime
6.31~\mathrm{SEC}
from
6
.
Depending on the outcome of EDM at the injection step, a Clifford
R_{y}(\pm\pi/2)
correction on the first logical qubit may be required.
We account for this by measuring
\bar{H}^{{}^{\prime}}\otimes\bar{H}
,
\bar{H}\otimes\bar{H}^{{}^{\prime}}
, or
\bar{H}^{{}^{\prime}}\otimes\bar{H}^{{}^{\prime}}
instead of
\bar{H}\otimes\bar{H}
, where
H^{{}^{\prime}}=R_{y}(-\pi/2)HR_{y}(\pi/2)
. This is possible since
H^{{}^{\prime}}=ZXH
.
On the symplectic basis of
Table

32
, the physical support of
\bar{X}_{i}
and
\bar{Z}_{i}
are identical, so it follows that to measure
\bar{H}^{{}^{\prime}}
on one or both logical qubits of
\mathrm{Q{54}}
, we replace the physical controlled-
H
of
Fig.

48
with controlled-
H^{{}^{\prime}}
on the support of their respective
\bar{X}
(and
\bar{Z}
) representatives.

Figure 49
:
\mathrm{CH2}
factory in the
\mathrm{Q{54}}
code, implemented in a code block with SEC-level scheduling. The circuit first applies the
H
-state injection gadget of
Fig.

45
to logical qubit
0
, then repeats the same
\mathrm{EDM}
-
3
injection on logical qubit
1
, where the required
\mathrm{EDM}
round count is estimated in
5
. It finally verifies the resulting candidate state by a transversal measurement of the joint logical observable
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
using
Fig.

48
, implemented as a Viterbi measurement with average runtime
6.31~\mathrm{SEC}
as estimated in
6
.

XV.4.2
Logical measurements

The
\mathrm{CH2}
schedule uses two measurement primitives: the hybrid
Y\bar{Y}

\mathrm{EDM}
inside each

H
-state injection gadget, and the final logical
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
Viterbi
measurement. For the hybrid
\mathrm{EDM}
we use logical-measurement error target

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{inj}}=10^{-5}
to choose the round count; the resulting
choice
r=3
already gives an estimated accepted-outcome error of order

10^{-8}
, numerically about
3.90\times 10^{-8}
by

5
. Larger
\mathrm{EDM}
round counts would only increase
retries. The purely logical Viterbi verification is instead run at the tighter
target
\varepsilon=10^{-10}
from
Section

XV.1
.

Heuristic 5

(Injection-
\mathrm{EDM}
round count for
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
)
.

For the
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
factory at the operating point

p=10^{-4}
and
p_{\log,\mathrm{\mathrm{Q{54}}}}=3\times 10^{-10}
,
choose the hybrid-injection
\mathrm{EDM}
round count

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}

\displaystyle=\min\bigl\{r\geq 1:

(22)

\displaystyle C_{2}p_{\log,\mathrm{\mathrm{Q{54}}}}+(C_{1}w_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}p)^{r}\leq 10^{-5}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=3,

with
w_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=16
.

Justification.

By
Figs.

40
and
18
, the injected logical
Y

can be represented with weight
16
in
\mathrm{Q{54}}
, so we take

w_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=16
for the hybrid
Y\bar{Y}
measurement in this
analysis. Using the
\mathrm{EDM}
heuristic

Eq.

16
with the fitted constants
C_{1}=2.1
and

C_{2}=3.4
from
Sections

XIII.3.1
and
XIII.3.2
,

\displaystyle C_{2}p_{\log,\mathrm{\mathrm{Q{54}}}}+(C_{1}w_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}p)^{2}

\displaystyle\approx 3.4\times 3\times 10^{-10}

\displaystyle+(2.1\times 16\times 10^{-4})^{2}

\displaystyle\approx 1.13\times 10^{-5}>10^{-5},

whereas

\displaystyle C_{2}p_{\log,\mathrm{\mathrm{Q{54}}}}+(C_{1}w_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}p)^{3}

\displaystyle\approx 3.4\times 3\times 10^{-10}

\displaystyle+(2.1\times 16\times 10^{-4})^{3}

\displaystyle\approx 3.90\times 10^{-8}<10^{-5}.

Hence the smallest admissible
\mathrm{EDM}
round count is

r_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=3
.
∎

Heuristic 6

(Viterbi runtime for
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
)
.

For the final transversal verification in the
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
factory, use the
average Viterbi runtime

\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},54}^{\mathrm{avg}}\approx 6.31~\mathrm{SEC},

(23)

corresponding to target logical-measurement error
\varepsilon=10^{-10}
.

Justification.

For the final verification, the measured observable is the transversal

\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
of weight
54
, so the weight-
54
,

\varepsilon=10^{-10}
entry of
Table

17
, obtained from the
Viterbi logical-measurement model of
Section

XIII.4
, gives the runtime estimate

Eq.

23
.
∎

XV.4.3
Error analysis

Under the ideal-logical-operation assumption of
Section

XV.1
, the only error sources for each
\mathrm{CH2}
attempt are the two injected
logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
states. The rest of the logical circuit is treated as perfect.

To produce a standard output pair of logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
states, we reject the
-1
outcome of the logical
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
measurement,
which corresponds to
|\bar{Y}\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{H}\rangle
or
|\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{Y}\bar{H}\rangle
.
We accept either
|\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{H}\rangle
or
|\bar{Y}\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{Y}\bar{H}\rangle
; the latter is an undetected logical error.
The
\mathrm{CH2}
error analysis therefore reduces to the chance that the two
\mathrm{EDM}
-
3

H
-state preparations from
5
each produce an
independent injected-
\bar{Y}
fault.

Heuristic 7

(Injected-state error estimate for
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
)
.

For the
\mathrm{CH2}
factory implemented in the
\mathrm{Q{54}}
block, taking
p=10^{-4}
,

r=r_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=3
from
5
,
and the
\mathrm{Q{54}}
SEC depth from
Table

11
, the injected-
\bar{Y}
-fault
probability per accepted logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
preparation is estimated by

\displaystyle q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}

\displaystyle\approx 1.93\times 10^{-4}+\frac{4\times 28.15\times 10^{-4}}{150}

(24)

\displaystyle\approx 2.68\times 10^{-4}.

Justification.

Applying
3
with

p=10^{-4}
and

r=r_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=3
from

5
, together with

\mathrm{Depth}(\mathrm{SEC})=d_{\mathrm{POC}}=1+8+10.10+8.05+1=28.15

from the
\mathrm{Q{54}}
SEC time budget in
Table

11
,
gives the heuristic estimate
Eq.

24
.
∎

Heuristic 8

(Verification acceptance probability for
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
)
.

Assuming the two injection gadgets fail independently and the remaining
logical operations in the factory are perfect, we estimate the
\mathrm{CH2}
verification/postselection acceptance probability by

a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}\approx(1-q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}})^{2}+q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}^{2}\approx 0.99946.

(25)

Justification.

We assume i.i.d. logical-
\bar{Y}
faults on each injected logical

|\bar{H}\rangle
state; this is consistent with

Eq.

24
, where
q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}
is obtained by specializing

3
to the
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
injection
gadget under the ideal-logical-operation approximation. The
\mathrm{CH2}
factory accepts
when there are either none,
or exactly two, logical-
\bar{Y}
faults. This gives the heuristic estimate

a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}\approx(1-q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}})^{2}+q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}^{2}.

Evaluating at the injected-state estimate
Eq.

24
gives the quoted
numerical value.
∎

Heuristic 9

(Output error estimate for the
\mathrm{CH2}
factory)
.

Conditioned on even-parity postselection, the
\mathrm{CH2}
output pair has estimated logical
error rate of

p_{\mathrm{CH2,out}}\approx\frac{q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}^{2}}{a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}}\approx 7.2\times 10^{-8}.

(26)

Justification.

The quantity
q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}^{2}
estimates the probability of
the bad double-fault event before acceptance. However,

p_{\mathrm{CH2,out}}
is the error rate
after we keep only the runs that pass the final
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}

check. That check rejects the odd-parity cases, so among accepted runs only
two branches remain: the good branch

|\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{H}\rangle
and the bad branch

|\bar{Y}\bar{H}\rangle|\bar{Y}\bar{H}\rangle
. The bad branch arises precisely when both
injections produce an injected
\bar{Y}
fault, which is estimated to occur with
probability
q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}^{2}
.
Therefore the output error is the fraction of accepted runs that lie in
this bad branch,

p_{\mathrm{CH2,out}}\approx\frac{\Pr[\text{bad and accepted}]}{\Pr[\text{accepted}]}\approx\frac{q_{Y,\mathrm{CH2}}^{2}}{a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}},

where
\Pr[\text{accepted}]\approx a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}
by

Eq.

25
. Evaluating at
Eq.

24

yields the stated numerical estimate.
∎

XV.4.4
Time analysis

We next convert the
\mathrm{CH2}
schedule shown in
Fig.

49
into an
average SEC cost. Apart from the initial logical
|0\rangle
preparation counted
below, one
\mathrm{CH2}
attempt consists exactly of the two
\mathrm{EDM}
-based injections and the
final transversal verification.

Heuristic 10

(Per-attempt failure rate estimate for the
\mathrm{CH2}
factory)
.

For the
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
implementation, the estimated per-attempt
failure rate is

p_{\mathrm{CH2,fail}}\approx 1-a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}\approx 2.28\%.

(27)

Justification.

For the
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
injection,

Figs.

40
and
18
show that the logical
Y

representative can be taken to have weight
16
, so we use
w=16
in the
retry estimate. Therefore,
4
together with the fitted
value
C_{1}=2.1
from
Section

XIII.3.1
,

r=r_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=3
from
5
,

p=10^{-4}
,
p_{\text{loss}}=10^{-7}
, and
p_{\text{leak}}=10^{-5}
gives

\displaystyle p_{\text{flip}}

\displaystyle\approx C_{1}wp=2.1\times 16\times 10^{-4}=3.36\times 10^{-3},

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{anc,det}}

\displaystyle\approx 4\times 28.15\times(10^{-7}+10^{-5})

\displaystyle=1.13726\times 10^{-3}\approx 1.14\times 10^{-3},

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle\approx 1

\displaystyle-\Bigl((1-3.36\times 10^{-3})^{3}

\displaystyle+(3.36\times 10^{-3})^{3}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times(1-1.14\times 10^{-3})

\displaystyle\approx 1.12\times 10^{-2}.

Hence, one injection is accepted with probability

a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}=1-p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\approx 0.9888
, so the
probability that both injections are accepted is

a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{2}\approx 0.9778.

By
Eq.

25
, the
\mathrm{CH2}
verification/postselection acceptance probability is

a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}\approx 0.99946
, so the per-attempt failure rate is

p_{\mathrm{CH2,fail}}\approx 1-a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}\approx 2.28\%
.
∎

Heuristic 11

(Average runtime estimate for the
\mathrm{CH2}
factory)
.

For the
\mathrm{CH2}
/
\mathrm{Q{54}}
implementation, the expected SEC count per successful
\mathrm{CH2}
output pair is

N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\approx 13.44~\mathrm{SEC},

(28)

or about
6.72~\mathrm{SEC}
per logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
state.

Justification.

Write
N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}
for the expected SEC count
until one successful
\mathrm{CH2}
output pair. A first-step decomposition over the
four possible outcomes of one
\mathrm{CH2}
attempt gives the recursive relation

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\left(D_{1}+N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\right)

\displaystyle+a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\left(D_{2}+N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\right)

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}(1-a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}})

\displaystyle\times\left(C_{\mathrm{CH2}}+N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\right)

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}C_{\mathrm{CH2}}.

Here
D_{1}
is the SEC cost if the first injection fails. Likewise,

D_{2}
is the cost if the second injection fails after the first succeeds.
Finally,
C_{\mathrm{CH2}}
is the SEC cost of a full
\mathrm{CH2}
schedule
through the final verification.

As shown in
Fig.

49
, after the initial logical
|0\rangle

preparation the factory consists only of two uses of the

H
-state injection gadget of
Fig.

45
followed by the
final transversal
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
verification. Each logical

|\bar{H}\rangle
preparation is implemented with an
\mathrm{EDM}
-
r_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}

measurement from
5
, so each injection attempt costs

N_{H\text{-inj}}=r_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}=3~\mathrm{SEC}.

The final verification is the weight-
54
Viterbi measurement from

Fig.

49
. By
6
, its
average runtime is

\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},54}^{\mathrm{avg}}=6.31~\mathrm{SEC}
. Since the
initial logical
|0\rangle
preparation costs
1~\mathrm{SEC}
, we have

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=1+N_{H\text{-inj}}=4~\mathrm{SEC},

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=1+2N_{H\text{-inj}}=7~\mathrm{SEC},

\displaystyle C_{\mathrm{CH2}}

\displaystyle=1+2N_{H\text{-inj}}+\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},54}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx 13.31~\mathrm{SEC},

and from the justification of
Eq.

27
,

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle\approx 1.12\times 10^{-2},

\displaystyle a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}

\displaystyle=1-p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\approx 0.9888,

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}

\displaystyle=a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{2}\approx 0.9778,

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.99946.

Substituting these values into the recursion, first collect the terms
proportional to
N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}
:

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}D_{1}

\displaystyle+a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}D_{2}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}C_{\mathrm{CH2}}

\displaystyle+\Bigl(p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}+a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}(1-a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}})\Bigr)N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}.

Since

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}+a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}(1-a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}})

\displaystyle=1-a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}},

this becomes

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}D_{1}

\displaystyle+a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}D_{2}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}C_{\mathrm{CH2}}

\displaystyle+(1-a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}})N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}.

Rearranging gives

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{CH2}}

\displaystyle=p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}D_{1}

\displaystyle+a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}D_{2}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}C_{\mathrm{CH2}},

so that

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx S_{\mathrm{CH2}},

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{S_{\mathrm{CH2}}}{a_{\mathrm{CH2,inj}}a_{\mathrm{CH2,ver}}}.

Numerically,

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{CH2}}

\displaystyle\approx(1.12\times 10^{-2})(4)

\displaystyle+(0.9888)(1.12\times 10^{-2})(7)

\displaystyle+(0.9778)(13.31)

\displaystyle\approx 13.14,

so

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{CH2,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{13.14}{0.9773}

\displaystyle\approx 13.44~\mathrm{SEC}.

Dividing by the two logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
outputs gives
6.72~\mathrm{SEC}
per
output state.
∎

XV.5
The MEK factory

The
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol
131
uses a
[[4,2,2]]
code to distill two injected
|\bar{H}\rangle
states.
The
[[4,2,2]]
code does not have a strongly transversal Hadamard, but does have a transversal Hadamard up to a swap of the two logical qubits.
The protocol therefore realizes the
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
verification through the more complex circuit of
Fig.

50
.

XV.5.1
The MEK factory circuit

The
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol leverages a transversal implementation of logical
\overline{\mathrm{SWAP}}\cdot\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
in the
[[4,2,2]]
error-detection code to verify two encoded logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
states.

Figure

50
shows the corresponding protocol circuit; therein, unitary encoding of two logical
H
states is followed by a verification measurement via a sequence of logical
CH
gates (see Ref.
131
and
Fig.

46
).
The distillation succeeds only if both the verification measurement as well as syndromes of the
[[4,2,2]]
indicate no error(s).
A unitary decoding then yields two verified
H
states. In our architecture, the
\mathrm{MEK}
factory hosts the entire
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol and its
[[4,2,2]]
code within a
\mathrm{Q{70}}
code block, so that the resulting logical
\bar{H}
states reside within a high-performance qLDPC memory.

This choice of code is also convenient for logical measurements: in the
\mathrm{Q{70}}
block, every logical Pauli observable on the six encoded qubits admits a representative of weight at most
18
; see
Figs.

39
and
18
. Hence all logical Clifford operations can be absorbed into Clifford frame tracking, so the highlighted operations in
Fig.

50
are the only steps that require physical implementation.

Figure

51
expands those operations, together with the final logical-qubit readout, into an IS+H-based schedule. Only five of the six logical qubits encoded by
\mathrm{Q{70}}
are needed to host the
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol, so the block effectively has one spare logical qubit. This spare qubit arises because BB and GB codes are built from a two-block group-algebra structure, which forces the number of encoded logical qubits to be even. The initial
{|\bar{H}\rangle}
states are prepared using the
\mathrm{EDM}
-
3
injection circuit of
Fig.

45
. Each logical controlled-
\bar{H}
gadget is decomposed into two
\mathrm{EDM}
-based logical
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
injections plus Clifford frame updates, and the intermediate logical
\mathrm{CZ}
is likewise absorbed into the frame. The only remaining purely logical Viterbi measurements are the three terminal readouts, each of weight at most
18
, timed using
13
. In total, the schedule uses ten injected states: two to prepare the initial logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
states and eight for the four controlled-
\bar{H}
gadgets, since each gadget contains two
R_{y}
gates.

Figure 50
:
Circuit for the
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol from Ref.
131
. The highlighted operations are the only steps that remain to be implemented as actual logical measurements once the Clifford part of the protocol is absorbed into frame updates.

Figure 51
:
\mathrm{MEK}
factory in the
\mathrm{Q{70}}
code, implemented in a code block with SEC-level scheduling. This schedule is obtained from
Fig.

50
by expanding each highlighted operation and the final logical qubit measurements into their IS+H-based implementation. Only five of the six logical qubits of
\mathrm{Q{70}}
are needed to host the
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol, leaving one spare logical qubit. This spare qubit is a consequence of the BB/GB two-block group-algebra construction, which forces the number of encoded logical qubits to be even.

XV.5.2
Logical measurements

As in
\mathrm{CH2}
, the MEK schedule uses two measurement primitives: the hybrid

Y\bar{Y}

\mathrm{EDM}
s inside the ten
H
-state injection gadgets, and the three
terminal purely logical Viterbi readouts. The
R_{y}(\pm\pi/4)
gates inside the
controlled-
\bar{H}
gadgets are therefore compiled entirely into
\mathrm{EDM}
-based
injections and Clifford frame updates. We again use

\varepsilon_{\mathrm{inj}}=10^{-5}
as the round-count target for the hybrid

\mathrm{EDM}
s; the resulting choice
r=3
gives an estimated accepted-outcome error
of order
10^{-8}
, numerically about
5.44\times 10^{-8}
by

12
. The terminal Viterbi measurements are run at the
target
\varepsilon=10^{-10}
of
Section

XV.1
.

Heuristic 12

(Injection-
\mathrm{EDM}
round count for
\mathrm{MEK}
/
\mathrm{Q{70}}
)
.

For the
\mathrm{MEK}
/
\mathrm{Q{70}}
factory at the operating point

p=10^{-4}
and
p_{\log,\mathrm{\mathrm{Q{70}}}}=10^{-10}
,
choose the hybrid-injection
\mathrm{EDM}
round count

\displaystyle r_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}

\displaystyle=\min\bigl\{r\geq 1:

(29)

\displaystyle C_{2}p_{\log,\mathrm{\mathrm{Q{70}}}}+(C_{1}w_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}p)^{r}\leq 10^{-5}\bigr\}

\displaystyle=3,

with
w_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=18
.

Justification.

By

and

, the injected logical
Y
can
be represented with weight
18
in
\mathrm{Q{70}}
. Thus

w_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=18
. Using

16
together with the fitted constants

C_{1}=2.1
and
C_{2}=3.4
from

XIII.3.1
and

,

\displaystyle C_{2}p_{\log,\mathrm{\mathrm{Q{70}}}}+(C_{1}w_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}p)^{2}

\displaystyle\approx 3.4\times 10^{-10}

\displaystyle+(2.1\times 18\times 10^{-4})^{2}

\displaystyle\approx 1.43\times 10^{-5}>10^{-5},

whereas

\displaystyle C_{2}p_{\log,\mathrm{Q70}}+(C_{1}w_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}p)^{3}

\displaystyle\approx 3.4\times 10^{-10}

\displaystyle+(2.1\times 18\times 10^{-4})^{3}

\displaystyle\approx 5.44\times 10^{-8}<10^{-5}.

Hence the smallest admissible
\mathrm{EDM}
round count is

r_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=3
.
∎

.

XV.5.3
Error analysis

Under the ideal-logical-operation approximation of
Section

XV.1
, the ten noisy
inputs to one execution of the
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol are the injected logical
|\bar{H}\rangle

states, while the rest of the logical circuit is treated as perfect.

Heuristic 14

(Injected-state error estimate for
\mathrm{MEK}
/
\mathrm{Q{70}}
)
.

For the
\mathrm{MEK}
factory implemented in the
\mathrm{Q{70}}
block, taking
p=10^{-4}
,

r=r_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=3
from
12
,
and the
\mathrm{Q{70}}
SEC depth from
Table

11
, the injected-
\bar{Y}
-fault
probability per accepted logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
preparation is estimated by

\displaystyle q_{Y,\mathrm{MEK}}

\displaystyle\approx 1.93\times 10^{-4}+\frac{4\times 27.70\times 10^{-4}}{150}

(31)

\displaystyle\approx 2.67\times 10^{-4}.

Justification.

Applying
3
with

p=10^{-4}
and

r=r_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=3
from

12
, together with

\mathrm{Depth}(\mathrm{SEC})=d_{\mathrm{POC}}=1+7+11.65+7.05+1=27.70

from the
\mathrm{Q{70}}
SEC time budget in
Table

11
,
gives the heuristic estimate
Eq.

31
.
∎

Heuristic 15

(Verification acceptance probability for
\mathrm{MEK}
/
\mathrm{Q{70}}
)
.

Model the ten logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
inputs to one
\mathrm{MEK}
attempt as independent
twirled resource states, each with stochastic
\bar{Y}
error probability
q
,
and assume the remaining logical operations in the factory are perfect.
Then, upon substituting the heuristic input value

q_{Y,\mathrm{MEK}}\approx 2.67\times 10^{-4}
, the
verification/postselection stage succeeds with probability

a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}(q_{Y,\mathrm{MEK}})\approx 0.99733.

(32)

Justification.

Sections II–III of Ref.
131
define the standard
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol acceptance event: the protocol decodes the outer
[[4,2,2]]
block and
accepts only if neither the encoded
\bar{H}^{\otimes 2}
measurement nor
the final syndrome indicates an error. Section III then gives the exact
expressions

\displaystyle a(q)

\displaystyle=1-10q+58q^{2}-192q^{3}+400q^{4}

(33)

\displaystyle-544q^{5}+480q^{6}-256q^{7}+64q^{8},

\displaystyle u(q)

\displaystyle=9q^{2}-56q^{3}+160q^{4}-256q^{5}

(34)

\displaystyle+240q^{6}-128q^{7}+32q^{8},

\displaystyle u_{2}(q)

\displaystyle=13q^{2}-80q^{3}+228q^{4}-368q^{5}

(35)

\displaystyle+352q^{6}-192q^{7}+48q^{8},

Here
a(q)
is the total acceptance probability of the
\mathrm{MEK}
protocol,

u(q)
is the accepted probability that a specified output state is bad,
and
u_{2}(q)
is the accepted probability that at least one of the two
output states is bad.
Section III also shows that every single input
\bar{Y}
fault is rejected. Let

P_{1}
denote the accepted probability that only output
1
is bad, let

P_{2}
denote the same event for output
2
, and let
P_{12}
denote the
accepted probability that both outputs are bad. By symmetry,

u(q)=P_{1}+P_{12}=P_{2}+P_{12},\qquad u_{2}(q)=P_{1}+P_{2}+P_{12}.

Therefore the odd-parity accepted branch, in which exactly one output is in

|\bar{Y}\bar{H}\rangle
, has probability

\displaystyle 2\bigl(u_{2}(q)-u(q)\bigr)

\displaystyle=8q^{2}-48q^{3}+136q^{4}-224q^{5}

(36)

\displaystyle+224q^{6}-128q^{7}+32q^{8},

so the branch that yields a standard pair of logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
states
has probability

a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}(q)=a(q)-2\bigl(u_{2}(q)-u(q)\bigr).

(37)

Evaluating at the injected-state estimate
Eq.

31
gives

2\bigl(u_{2}(q_{Y,\mathrm{MEK}})-u(q_{Y,\mathrm{MEK}})\bigr)\approx 5.70\times 10^{-7}
, together with

Eq.

32
.
∎

Heuristic 16

(Output error estimate for the
\mathrm{MEK}
factory)
.

Conditioned on the successful branch counted by

Eq.

32
, each
\mathrm{MEK}
output state has estimated error

p_{\mathrm{MEK,out}}\approx e_{\mathrm{even}}(q_{Y,\mathrm{MEK}})\approx 3.6\times 10^{-7}.

(38)

Justification.

In the same idealized model, the even-parity bad branch has probability

P_{12}=2u(q)-u_{2}(q)
, so the conditional per-output-state error is

e_{\mathrm{even}}(q)=\frac{2u(q)-u_{2}(q)}{a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}(q)}.

(39)

Evaluating
Eq.

39
at the heuristic input value
from
Eq.

31
, and using
Eq.

32
, gives

Eq.

38
.
∎

XV.5.4
Time analysis

We now translate the expanded logical schedule of

Fig.

51
into an SEC cost.

Heuristic 17

(Per-attempt failure rate estimate for the
\mathrm{MEK}
factory)
.

Assuming the ten injection
\mathrm{EDM}
outcomes are independent, the

\mathrm{MEK}
/
\mathrm{Q{70}}
implementation has estimated per-attempt failure rate

p_{\mathrm{MEK,fail}}\approx 1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}\,a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}\approx 11.97\%.

(40)

Justification.

The first source of failure is the ten
\mathrm{EDM}
-
3

H
-state injections from

12
. As in
the
\mathrm{CH2}
analysis, we model each injection restart with

4
. For the
\mathrm{Q{70}}
implementation,

Figs.

39
and
18
show that logical
Y

representatives have weight at most
18
.
Therefore, using the fitted value
C_{1}=2.1
from

Section

XIII.3.1
, together with

r=r_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=3
from
12
,

p=10^{-4}
,
p_{\text{loss}}=10^{-7}
, and
p_{\text{leak}}=10^{-5}
,

\displaystyle p_{\text{flip}}

\displaystyle\approx C_{1}wp=2.1\times 18\times 10^{-4}=3.78\times 10^{-3},

\displaystyle p_{\mathrm{anc,det}}

\displaystyle\approx 4\times 27.70\times(10^{-7}+10^{-5})

\displaystyle=1.11908\times 10^{-3}\approx 1.12\times 10^{-3},

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle\approx 1-\Bigl((1-p_{\text{flip}})^{3}

\displaystyle+p_{\text{flip}}^{3}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\times(1-p_{\mathrm{anc,det}})

\displaystyle\approx 1.24\times 10^{-2}.

Hence one injection is accepted with probability

a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}=1-p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\approx 0.9876,

and therefore all ten injections succeed with probability

a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{10}\approx 0.883.

The second source of failure is rejection at the
\mathrm{MEK}
verification/postselection stage. By

Eq.

32
,

a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}\approx a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}(q_{Y,\mathrm{MEK}})\approx 0.99733.

Combining the ten injection acceptances with this verification/postselection
success probability gives

p_{\mathrm{MEK,fail}}\approx 1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}\approx 11.97\%
,
which is the heuristic estimate
Eq.

40
.
∎

Heuristic 18

(Average runtime estimate for the
\mathrm{MEK}
factory)
.

For the
\mathrm{MEK}
/
\mathrm{Q{70}}
implementation, taking the logical-measurement parameters
from
12
and
13
, the expected SEC count per
accepted
\mathrm{MEK}
output pair is

N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\approx 47.6~\mathrm{SEC},

(41)

or about
23.8~\mathrm{SEC}
per logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
state.

Justification.

Write
N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}
for the expected SEC count
until one successful
\mathrm{MEK}
output pair. A first-step decomposition over the
three possible outcomes of one
\mathrm{MEK}
attempt gives

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx\sum_{j=1}^{10}a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{j-1}p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle\times\left(D_{j}+N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\right)

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}(1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}})

\displaystyle\times\left(C_{\mathrm{MEK}}+N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}\right)

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}C_{\mathrm{MEK}}.

Here
D_{j}
is the accrued SEC count if the
j
th injection is the first one
to fail, and
C_{\mathrm{MEK}}
is the SEC cost of a complete
\mathrm{MEK}
schedule
up to and including the final logical readouts.

Each logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
preparation is implemented with an

\mathrm{EDM}
-
r_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}
measurement from

12
, so

I=r_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}=3~\mathrm{SEC}.

For the terminal Viterbi measurements in
Fig.

51
, we use the
conservative runtime estimate from
13
and
set

V=\tau_{\mathrm{Vit},20}^{\mathrm{avg}}=5.10~\mathrm{SEC}.

One successful
\mathrm{MEK}
schedule uses ten logical
|\bar{H}\rangle

preparations in total: two standalone injections, plus two injections
inside each of the four logical controlled-
\bar{H}
gadgets.
Only the three terminal logical readouts require Viterbi measurements.
Therefore

C_{\mathrm{MEK}}=10I+3V\approx 45.3~\mathrm{SEC}.

Index the ten logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
preparations in

Fig.

51
by the order in which their
H
-state
injection gadgets are executed. Since all ten injections occur before the
three terminal readouts, if the
j
th injection is the first one to fail
then the accrued SEC cost is simply
jI
. Therefore

\displaystyle D_{1}

\displaystyle=I,

\displaystyle D_{2}

\displaystyle=2I,

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle=3I,

\displaystyle D_{4}

\displaystyle=4I,

\displaystyle D_{5}

\displaystyle=5I,

\displaystyle D_{6}

\displaystyle=6I,

\displaystyle D_{7}

\displaystyle=7I,

\displaystyle D_{8}

\displaystyle=8I,

\displaystyle D_{9}

\displaystyle=9I,

\displaystyle D_{10}

\displaystyle=10I.

and from the justification of
Eq.

40
,

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}

\displaystyle\approx 1.24\times 10^{-2},

\displaystyle a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}

\displaystyle=1-p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\approx 0.9876,

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.883,

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}

\displaystyle\approx 0.99733.

Substituting these values into the recursion, first collect the terms
proportional to
N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}
:

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\sum_{j=1}^{10}a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{j-1}D_{j}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}C_{\mathrm{MEK}}

\displaystyle+p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\sum_{j=1}^{10}a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{j-1}

\displaystyle\times N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}(1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}})N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}.

Since

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\sum_{j=1}^{10}a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{j-1}

\displaystyle=1-a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{10}=1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}},

\displaystyle p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\sum_{j=1}^{10}a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{j-1}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}(1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}})

\displaystyle=(1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}})

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}(1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}})

\displaystyle=1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}},

this becomes

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\sum_{j=1}^{10}a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{j-1}D_{j}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}C_{\mathrm{MEK}}

\displaystyle+(1-a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}})N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}.

Rearranging gives

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{MEK}}

\displaystyle=p_{H,\mathrm{retry}}\sum_{j=1}^{10}a_{H,\mathrm{acc}}^{j-1}D_{j}

\displaystyle+a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}C_{\mathrm{MEK}},

so that

\displaystyle a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx S_{\mathrm{MEK}},

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{S_{\mathrm{MEK}}}{a_{\mathrm{MEK,inj}}a_{\mathrm{MEK,ver}}}.

Numerically,

\displaystyle S_{\mathrm{MEK}}

\displaystyle\approx(1.24\times 10^{-2})\sum_{j=1}^{10}(0.9876)^{j-1}D_{j}

\displaystyle+(0.883)(45.3)

\displaystyle\approx 41.88,

so

\displaystyle N_{\mathrm{MEK,SEC}}^{\mathrm{avg}}

\displaystyle\approx\frac{41.88}{(0.883)(0.99733)}

\displaystyle\approx 47.6~\mathrm{SEC}.

Dividing by the two logical
|\bar{H}\rangle
outputs gives

23.8~\mathrm{SEC}
per output state.
∎

XVI
The qubit factory

The walking cat architecture comprises multiple copies each of four distinct types of subsystems, denoted M (memory blocks), T (magic factories), C (Cat factories), and B (Bell factories).
During each SEC, every subsystem independently experiences qubit losses.

To maintain continuous operation and ease the burden of reloading qubits, we utilize a global reservoir, referred to in this section simply as the reservoir, of maximum capacity
R
that replenishes the qubits lost across all copies of M, T, C, and B. Concurrently,
L
loading zones each produce a new qubit at an average rate of one per second to refill the reservoir. We assume that a critical system failure occurs if the aggregate qubit loss across all subsystems during an SEC exceeds the current available inventory in the reservoir, implying that at least one subsystem will be unable to replace its lost qubits.

Our goal in this section is to determine a minimum reservoir size that guarantees the long-term probability of such a system failure remains below a chosen threshold
\epsilon=10^{-10}
, given a specific number of loading zones and an exact allocation of the subsystems.
In
Table

22
, we report values of
L
and
R
for which the reservoir will operate at this threshold for two different allocations when using
\mathrm{Q{102}}
for memory and
\mathrm{Q{54}}
for the magic factory.
These values are chosen from
Fig.

52
; the particular values we report in
Table

22
represent a point of diminishing returns where increasing the number of loading zones offers a minimal reduction in required reservoir size. This demonstrates that continuous system operation can be maintained in the presence of qubit loss, with a reasonable reservoir size and number of loading zones.

In
Section

XVI.1
, we introduce a discrete-time Markov chain model for the reservoir, the qubit loss distributions we assume, and the method for determining operating values of
L
and
R
needed to maintain at most
\epsilon
failure probability.

#M

#T

#C

#B

L

R

20

20

40

5

28

188

5

5

10

2

15

139

Table 22
:
Choices for number of qubit loading zones
L
and reservoir size
R
to operate below the critical system failure probability
\epsilon=10^{-10}
for two different allocations of the numbers of memory blocks (
#M
),
T
factories (
#T
), cat factories (
#C
), and Bell factories (
#B
). We assume here that the memory code is
\mathrm{Q{102}}
and that the magic factory code is
\mathrm{Q{54}}
.

XVI.1
Discrete-time Markov chain model

Figure 52
:
Reservoir Capacity (
R
) vs. Number of Loading Zones (
L
).
This figure displays the minimum required reservoir capacity
R
to maintain a system failure probability of
\leq 10^{-10}
across a varying number of loading zones
L
, and for different allocations of M and T blocks. We assume that each M and T block has an associated C block, so #C
=
#M+#T. For simplicity, we take #B
=\lceil
#C/9
\rceil
.
The asymptotic vertical behavior indicates the minimum number of loading zones required to maintain operation above the system failure threshold; asymptotic horizontal behavior shows that a minimum of 120 qubits are needed to avoid system failure, consistent with the case where 2 C components (containing 60 qubits each) each lose all of their qubits in a given SEC.

Figure 53
:
For each of the subsystem allocations from
Fig.

52
, we consider how the probability of system failure changes as the chosen operating point values of
L
loading zones and reservoir size
R
are perturbed by up to
50\%
each. Isolines on the 2D plots correspond to configurations of
L
and
R
that achieve an order of magnitude deviation from
\epsilon=10^{-10}
.

To determine a reservoir size and number of loading zones needed to operate below critical failure, we model the occupancy of the reservoir after each SEC as a discrete-time Markov chain. The state space is defined as the set of integers from
0
to
R
, representing the current number of available qubits.
In the context of our system, the steady state distribution of this Markov chain represents the equilibrium behavior of the reservoir after an infinite number of SECs. Given a configuration of
L
and
R
, we approximate system failure as the steady-state probability that the reservoir is empty.

To model the system, we assume the following:

•

Discrete Time Steps:
The system dynamics are evaluated at discrete intervals corresponding to the fixed SEC time,
\Delta t
. All loss and reloading events are aggregated at these time steps. Losses in the components are governed by subsystem-specific probability mass functions,
p^{(S)}_{k}
, representing the probability that a single subsystem of type
S\in\{\text{M, T, C, B}\}
loses exactly
k
qubits during one SEC.

•

Aggregated Loss Distribution:
The total number of qubits lost in a single SEC is the sum of the losses from all individual subsystems. Because the individual subsystems operate independently, the aggregate loss probability distribution,
P_{\text{total loss}}
, can be computed as the convolution of the individual subsystem distributions.

•

Binary Loading:
After each SEC, the
L
loading zones collectively add either zero or one qubit. As loading zones add approximately 1 qubit per second each to the reservoir, in the model we assume that after a single SEC a single qubit is added with probability
P_{\mathrm{add}}(1)=L\cdot\Delta t
. The total stored qubits cannot exceed the reservoir capacity
R
.

Denote the random variable of the state of the reservoir at time step
t
by
X_{t}
. During a single SEC, let
k
be the number of qubits lost across all subsystems, governed by the convolved probability distribution
P_{\mathrm{loss}}(k)
. Let
a
be the number of qubits added, governed by the binary distribution
P_{\mathrm{add}}(a)
where
a\in\{0,1\}
. The value of the system at time step
t+1
is given by

X_{t+1}=\max(0,\min(R,X_{t}-k+a)).

The transition matrix
P
of size
(R+1)\times(R+1)
is constructed by marginalizing over all possible loss and addition events; the transition probability from state
i
to state
j
is given by

P_{i,j}=\sum_{k,a\colon X_{i+1}=j}P_{\text{total loss}}(k)\cdot P_{\mathrm{add}}(a).

The steady-state distribution vector
\pi
describes the long-term, time-independent probability of finding the reservoir in any given state, and is given by the normalized left eigenvector of the transition matrix
P
corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda=1
.
The probability of critical system failure corresponds to the first element of the distribution vector,
\pi_{0}
. We require this failure probability to be strictly less than the critical system failure threshold,
\pi_{0}<\epsilon=10^{-10}
.

For the numerical simulations of this section, we make the following choices:

•

The loss distribution
p_{k}^{(M)}
for the memory blocks is taken to be the distribution for
\mathrm{Q{102}}
reported in
Table

11
.

•

The loss distribution
p_{k}^{(T)}
for the T factories is taken to be the distribution for
\mathrm{Q{54}}
reported in
Table

11
.

•

The loss distribution
p_{k}^{(C)}
for the cat factories is taken to be the distribution from
Table

13
with weight
w=30
,
m=2
verification rounds, and
p_{\mathrm{loss}}=10^{-7}
. The cat factories associated with the magic factories could, in principle, produce smaller-weight cat states, so this choice is pessimistic.

•

The loss distribution
p_{k}^{(B)}
for the Bell factories is taken to have only two non-trivial contributions
p_{2}^{(B)}=3.6\times 10^{-6}
and
p_{0}^{(B)}=1-p_{2}^{(B)}
,
i.e.
, we suppose that with probability
p_{2}^{(B)}
the Bell factory fails to produce a Bell state.

•

We assume an SEC time of
\Delta t=6\times 10^{-3}
, so the probability of successfully adding a qubit at the end of a single SEC is taken to be
P_{\mathrm{add}}(1)=0.006L
.

Because the probability of an empty reservoir strictly decreases when increasing the maximum reservoir capacity
R
for a fixed value of
L
, we compute the minimal required capacity for a given number of loading zones using binary search. We define a search space between a minimum capacity of
1
and a predefined maximum practical limit
R_{\max}=500
. By fixing an
L
and iteratively computing the failure probability for the value of
R
at the midpoint of the search space, we converge on the smallest reservoir size for which the system failure probability remains below
\epsilon
for the given number of loading zones.

In
Fig.

52
, we report the relationship between
L
and
R
operating at this critical threshold
\epsilon
for different allocation and loss rate scenarios. For each of these scenarios, we choose the operating values of
L
and
R
for which increasing
L
by one only decreases the required reservoir size by two qubits. This choice is simply meant to represent a point at which adding more loading zones does not result in a drastic reduction of reservoir size; any point on the curve would maintain continuous operation below the
\epsilon
threshold. In
Fig.

53
, we examine the effect on the total system failure probability when perturbing the values for this operating point.

To validate our DTMC model, we demonstrate rapid convergence of the reservoir state to the steady-state distribution in
Fig.

54
.

Figure 54
:
1-norm distance to the steady-state distribution vs. the number of SECs.
By
\approx 250
SECs, both distributions are within
1\%
of the steady state.

:

10^{-4}
using a
(5,3)
sliding window beam search decoder. Reaction time is the interval between the final qubit measurement and the acquisition of the final decoding result. Decoding time statistics are derived from a single run of
1,000,008

10,000
